=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a 2D array queries, where queries[i] is of the form [l, r]. Each queries[i] defines an array of integers nums consisting of elements ranging from l to r, both inclusive.
In one operation, you can:

Select two integers a and b from the array.
Replace them with floor(a / 4) and floor(b / 4).

Your task is to determine the minimum number of operations required to reduce all elements of the array to zero for each query. Return the sum of the results for all queries.
 
Example 1:

Input: queries = [[1,2],[2,4]]
Output: 3
Explanation:
For queries[0]:

The initial array is nums = [1, 2].
In the first operation, select nums[0] and nums[1]. The array becomes [0, 0].
The minimum number of operations required is 1.

For queries[1]:

The initial array is nums = [2, 3, 4].
In the first operation, select nums[0] and nums[2]. The array becomes [0, 3, 1].
In the second operation, select nums[1] and nums[2]. The array becomes [0, 0, 0].
The minimum number of operations required is 2.

The output is 1 + 2 = 3.

Example 2:

Input: queries = [[2,6]]
Output: 4
Explanation:
For queries[0]:

The initial array is nums = [2, 3, 4, 5, 6].
In the first operation, select nums[0] and nums[3]. The array becomes [0, 3, 4, 1, 6].
In the second operation, select nums[2] and nums[4]. The array becomes [0, 3, 1, 1, 1].
In the third operation, select nums[1] and nums[2]. The array becomes [0, 0, 0, 1, 1].
In the fourth operation, select nums[3] and nums[4]. The array becomes [0, 0, 0, 0, 0].
The minimum number of operations required is 4.

The output is 4.

 
Constraints:

1 <= queries.length <= 10^5
queries[i].length == 2
queries[i] == [l, r]
1 <= l < r <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's craft code:

from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_right? Maybe not needed if using prefix_and_level.

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        def prefix_and_level(n: int):
            total = 0
            level = 0
            p = 1
            while p <= n:
                total += n - p + 1
                level += 1
                p *= 4
            return total, level

        ans = 0
        for l, r in queries:
            sr, mr = prefix_and_level(r)
            sl, _ = prefix_and_level(l - 1)
            s = sr - sl
            ans += max((s + 1) // 2, mr)
        return ans

Now, let's examine if the min operations formula can be derived from "each element with h_i needs h_i operations; each operation can serve two elements" but max h_i lower bound. from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_right

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        powers = [1]
        while powers[-1] <= 10**9:
            powers.append(powers[-1] * 4)
        
        def pref(n: int) -> int:
            k = bisect_right(powers, n)
            return k * (n + 1) - (powers[k] - 1) // 3
        
        ans = 0
        for l, r in queries:
            total = pref(r) - pref(l - 1)
            max_level = bisect_right(powers, r)
            ans += max((total + 1) // 2, max_level)
        return ans

If k=0, sum empty =0, (1-1)/3=0. Good.

Let's maybe attempt to prove lower bound more formally:
Let c_i be number of operations that include element i. Since each operation includes an element at most once, c_i >= h_i? Actually to reduce element i to zero, it must be selected exactly h_i times (unless selected when zero, which doesn't change h but counts; extra selections unnecessary). So c_i >= h_i. Since each operation has two selections, total selections = sum c_i >= S, so number operations T >= ceil(S/2). Also for any i, T >= c_i >= h_i, so T >= max h_i. Thus T >= max(ceil(S/2), max h_i). Good.

Sufficiency:
We can use McNaughton. But maybe there is a simpler constructive argument for two machines:
Given T = max(max h_i, ceil(S/2)). We can schedule by arranging all h_i unit tasks in a sequence of length S, then cutting it into blocks of length T? Wait if we arrange tasks in any order and assign first T tasks to operation 1? Let's think:
We have S unit tasks, each labeled by element. Need group them into T operations, each operation has at most two tasks, and no operation contains two tasks of same element. If we sort tasks arbitrarily and group into pairs sequentially, a group could contain same element twice (e.g., h=[3,1], tasks A,A,A,B; grouping sequential pairs: (A,A) invalid, (A,B) okay). Need avoid same element in same group. Is there always a way if max h <= T and S<=2T? Yes, by scheduling.

Could use a greedy algorithm: At each operation, pick two elements with largest remaining h. Does this always finish in T? Let's test maybe. But not needed. However, if we want to be absolutely sure formula sufficient, let's try to find a counterexample to greedy or formula. We can brute force mentally for small T? Let's formalize as edge coloring of multigraph? Actually schedule is a multigraph between elements and time slots. Existence conditions for bipartite multigraph with left degrees h_i and right degrees at most 2, no multiple edges between same element and same time slot. This is a bipartite degree sequence problem. Gale-Ryser for bipartite graphs with left degrees h_i sorted nonincreasing and right degrees all 2 (some 1/0) says there exists a simple bipartite graph iff sum h_i = sum right degrees and for all subsets? Since right degrees are 2, the condition might be max h_i <= T and sum h_i <= 2T. Let's verify with Gale-Ryser: For bipartite graph with left degrees h_i (sorted descending) and right degrees d_j (all 2), the Gale-Ryser theorem says h is majorized by conjugate of d. Let's derive prefix formula in code:
def pref(n: int) -> int:
    k = (n.bit_length() + 1) // 2
    return k * (n + 1) - ((1 << (2 * k)) - 1) // 3

For n=0, k=0, 1 << 0 =1, (1-1)//3=0, k*(n+1)=0. Good.
For n=1, k=1, 1<<2=4, (4-1)//3=1, 1*2-1=1.
For n=2, k=1, 1*3-1=2.
For n=3, 1*4-1=3.
For n=4, k=2, 1<<4=16, (16-1)//3=5, 2*5-5=5.
Good.

This is very concise. Need ensure pref(l-1) for l=1: pref(0) returns 0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility to reduce an element's h by more than 1 in one operation by selecting it as both a and b? The operation says select two integers a and b from the array. If the array has only one occurrence of value x, can you select the same integer twice? Usually no, "two integers from the array" means two elements, distinct positions. Even if you could select the same value twice if it appears once, replacing with floor(a/4) and floor(b/4) would produce two copies? Not intended. We assume distinct elements. If same element could be selected twice, then a single operation could reduce one element by two levels if you choose a=b? But "two integers a and b from the array" in programming problems means choose two elements, not necessarily distinct? Usually "select two integers" might allow same value? But if there is only one occurrence, can't select it twice. If there are two occurrences, selecting both is distinct. Our model uses distinct elements. Good.

Let's maybe search memory: LeetCode problem "Minimum Operations to Make Array Zero" maybe operation: "Choose two different indices i and j" maybe not in prompt? Prompt says "Select two integers a and b from the array." It doesn't explicitly say different indices, but "two integers from the array" implies two elements. If the array contains two equal numbers, selecting "a and b" with same value could refer to two occurrences. You cannot select same element twice. So our model correct.

Need ensure no syntax errors:
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        def pref(n: int) -> int:
            k = (n.bit_length() + 1) // 2
            return k * (n + 1) - ((1 << (2 * k)) - 1) // 3

        ans = 0
        for l, r in queries:
            total = pref(r) - pref(l - 1)
            max_level = (r.bit_length() + 1) // 2
            ans += max((total + 1) // 2, max_level)
        return ans

Let's test pref(0): k=(0+1)//2=0; 1 << 0 =1; return 0*1 -0=0. Good.
pref(1): k=1; 1<<2=4; return 1*2 -1=1.
pref(2): k=1; 1*3 -1=2.
pref(3): 1*4 -1=3.
pref(4): k=2; 1<<4=16; 2*5 -5=5.
pref(15): k=2; 2*16 -5=27.
pref(16): k=3; 1<<6=64; 3*17 -21=30.
pref(268435455): bit_length? 268435455 < 2^28=268435456, >=2^27=134217728, bit_length=28, k=14, 1<<28=268435456, (268435456-1)//3=89478485, prefix=14*268435456 - 89478485. Let's compute expected: levels 1..14. Sum =? 14*(n+1) - sum powers 0..13. n+1=268435456. 14*268435456=3,758,096,384. Sum powers 0..13=(4^14-1)/3=(268,435,456-1)/3=89,478,485. Difference=3,668,617,899. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "minimum number of operations" if we can select two integers and one is zero, the zero remains, so if we need an idle slot before any zero, invalid. We argued no. But let's test a schedule from formula where first operation might need idle if S<T? We argued S>=T. For valid arrays, S>=T. Let's prove S>=T:
T = max(M, ceil(S/2)).
If T=ceil(S/2), then S>=ceil(S/2) for S>=0. For S=0, equal; but S>0. So S>=T.
If T=M, then S>=M because there is at least one element with h=M. So S>=T.
Thus S>=T. So in any schedule with T operations, total capacity 2T, idle slots 2T-S <= T. If S=T, idle slots T (machine2 all idle). Could S=T with valid l<r? S>=m>=2 and M<=S. If S=T=M, then all h sum equals max, so all other elements h=0, impossible because all elements positive. Thus S>T. So idle slots < T. But could first operation need idle? If S>T, there are at least T+1 selections over T ops, so by pigeonhole at least one op has two selections. But first op could still be idle if we schedule badly; we can choose schedule with first op two selections because there are at least two positive elements initially. Since all elements positive and m>=2, first op can select two positives. Good. So zero availability not a problem. Good.
